As for the matter of his statement: if one were to accept it as true, it was the tale, common enough two centuries ago, but so rarely told now that modern ears find it very hard to take it in, the tale of the ordinary struggle between good and evil in a man, taking an acute and violent form, so that the man feels day by day the alternate mastery of a religious exaltation, which he believes to be wholly good, and of base passions, which, when they come upon him, seem to be an evil spirit driving him as the steam drives an engine. From the manner of the statement, it was very hard to gather how much of it was sincere, impossible to gather whether or not something worse lay concealed behind that which was so strangely confessed. Self-abasement and self-righteousness, the genuine stuff of Puritan enthusiasm, the adulterated stuff of morbid religiousness, sheer cant, manly straightforwardness, pleasure in the opportunity of preaching and that to the parson—all these things seemed blended together in Trethewy's talk.

===Chinese===
至于他陈述的内容：倘若信以为真，那便是这样一个故事——两百年前颇为常见，如今却极少有人讲述，以致现代人的耳朵很难将它听进去——一个人身上善与恶的寻常争战，只是采取了尖锐而暴烈的形式，于是这个人日复一日地感到，自己轮番受着两样东西的辖制：一是他相信全然为善的宗教性激扬，一是卑贱的情欲；情欲一旦袭来，便仿佛一个恶灵驱策着他，恰似蒸汽驱策引擎。从陈述的姿态来看，很难判断其中有几分真诚，更不可能判断在那番离奇供认的背后，是否还潜藏着更不堪的东西。自我贬抑与自以为义，清教热忱的真材实料，病态宗教情绪的掺假货色，十足的伪善，男子汉的坦直，还有逮住布道机会的快意——而且是布道给牧师听——这一切似乎都混杂在特雷瑟维的话语里。

===Original===
On the most favourable view the story came to this. A few years before, Trethewy, after a careless life, had become suddenly impressed by deep religious feelings, no less than by precise and inflexible religious views. His conversion, he trusted, had not left his conduct unaffected, but though for a time he walked, as he said, happy in this new light, it had been the beginning, not the end, of his inward warfare. His natural ill-temper, that worst sort of ill-temper which is both sulky and passionate, began to come upon him again in prolonged fits of intense wrath, intensified, I suppose, by reaction from the pitch at which he often strove to live. Besides this, he gave way at times to a keen pleasure in alcohol. He was tempted by what he called a "carnal" pride in the strength of his head for liquor; and I have sometimes observed that drink works its worst havoc upon the very men who may appear to be the least affected by it, bringing about a slow perversion of the deeper motives of action, while for a long time it leaves the judgment unclouded upon those more trivial and obvious matters in which aberration is readily detected. Thus at the time of that altercation with Peters of which Callaghan had been a witness, Trethewy was already brooding perversely over some trumpery or altogether fancied grievance. He was deeply under the influence of drink at that moment, and did not know it, but knew he had had enough to make most men drunk. His very worldly pride had therefore been the more offended at the imputation which Peters threw on him. His spiritual pride was offended too by a rebuke from one, whom, though originally fond of him, he had come to regard as a worldling, steeped in mere profane philosophy. He had been enraged to the point of desiring Peters' death, and the threat which Callaghan reported had been actually uttered. He had meant, it may be, nearly nothing by his threat when he uttered it; but, when once this almost insane notion, of killing for such a trifle a man whom normally he liked, had taken shape in words, it recurred to him every time that he was put out, or that a third glass of spirits went to his lips. Perhaps it recurred to him with all the more terrible power because in better moments his conscience was horribly alarmed at his having given in, by so much as one thought, to this suggestion of the Devil. On the morning before Peters' death he had a fresh altercation with him on the occasion of some trifling oversight in the garden to which Peters had called his attention, and I was surprised after what Vane-Cartwright had said to be told that Vane-Cartwright was present on this occasion and had heard the insolent language in which he seems to have addressed Peters. All day and night after that the evil dream had been upon him, and he walked home from his uncle's that night plotting murder. He awoke in the morning calmer, but his wrath still smouldered, till his wife brought him the news that Peters was murdered, when it gave place in a moment to poignant grief for Peters. He could not stir from the cottage; he sat, he tried to pray, he thought, and he saw himself as he was—perhaps not quite as he was, for he saw himself as a man guilty of blood.

===Chinese===
往最宽厚处看，这故事的原委如下。数年之前，特雷瑟维在过了一段漫不经心的日子之后，忽然被深沉的宗教情感所触动，同时也接受了一套精确而不容变通的宗教见解。他相信，这场皈依并非不曾影响他的品行；然而，虽说有一段时日，如他自己所言，他行走在这新的光照之中，满心喜乐，但那皈依只是他内心争战的开始，而非终结。他天生的坏脾气——那种最坏的坏脾气，既阴郁又暴烈——又开始以一阵阵延绵的盛怒向他袭来；我猜想，这是由于他常常勉力维持一种过分高昂的生活状态，随后的反作用便使怒火愈发炽烈。除此之外，他还不时沉溺于饮酒的强烈快感。一种诱惑引动着他：他为自己经得起酒、头脑不乱的本事怀有一种自豪，他称之为“肉体的”骄傲。而我有时观察到，酒为祸最烈的，恰恰是那些看上去最不受它影响的人：它使行动的深层动机缓缓败坏，而在很长一段时间里，对于那些琐屑显见、一有失常便会立刻被人察觉的事务，它却让判断力保持清明。因此，在那一场卡拉汉曾亲眼目睹的、他与彼得斯的口角发生之时，特雷瑟维早已在心里乖张地翻搅着某件鸡毛蒜皮、甚或全属凭空的怨艾。那一刻他其实已深陷酒力的影响，自己却不知道；他只知道，喝下的分量已足以醉倒大多数人。于是，彼得斯甩给他的那项指摘，便格外刺伤了他那十足的世俗骄傲。他的灵性骄傲也受了冒犯——斥责他的那个人，他起初原本喜欢，后来却渐渐视为一个十足的俗人，浸透在纯粹的亵渎哲学里。他曾狂怒到渴望彼得斯死的地步，卡拉汉转述的那句威胁，也确确实实说出口过。那句话出口之时，他或许并没当真存着什么意思；然而，一旦这个近乎疯狂的念头——为这样一点小事去杀一个平日里他并不讨厌的人——成了形，落进了言语，那么每逢他心头不快，或是第三杯烈酒举到唇边，这念头便会卷土重来。也许正因如此，它卷土重来时才带着愈发可怕的力量：因为在神志较清明的时刻，他的良心为自己竟向魔鬼的这个暗示让了步——哪怕只让过一个念头——而惊恐万分。彼得斯遇害的前一天早晨，园子里出了点小小的疏漏，彼得斯唤他注意，两人又起了一场新的口角。听到说韦恩-卡特赖特当时也在场、听到了他似乎用以对彼得斯讲的那番无礼言语，再想起韦恩-卡特赖特先前说过的话，我不由得暗暗吃惊。自那以后，那个罪恶的梦魇整日整夜缠着他；那天夜里，他从叔叔家走回家的路上，一路都在谋划杀人。清晨醒来，他平静了些，怒火却仍在阴燃，直到妻子带来彼得斯遇害的消息，那怒火才在一瞬间化作对彼得斯锥心的哀痛。他无法迈出小屋一步；他坐着，试图祷告，他思索，他看清了自己的本相——或许还不完全是他本来的样子，因为他把自己看成了一个手上沾着血的人。

===Original===
He would gladly, I think, have talked with me of his soul, but, with the suspicion which I had in my mind, I did not see how I could say much to him. So, having heard him out, I got away with some pitifully perfunctory remarks. How was I to take this confession? Was the mental history which the man gave of himself a cunning invention for accounting for the known quarrel and the known threats? Was the story true with this grave correction that Trethewy had carried out his intent? Was it the simple truth all through? Did it even go beyond the truth in this, that the man's thoughts had never been so black as he made them out? For days these questions occurred frequently to my mind, but my real opinion upon them was fixed almost as soon as I got away from Trethewy. Contrary to my principles I disliked him, I felt strangely little sympathy for his spiritual struggles; but I did not doubt that they were real, and I did not doubt that he was innocent of the crime.

===Chinese===
我想，他本会很乐意同我谈谈他的灵魂，可我心里既已存着怀疑，便看不出还能对他说多少话。于是，听他讲完之后，我丢下几句可怜地敷衍了事的话，便脱身走开了。这番供认，我该如何看待？此人对自己心智历程的自述，会不会是一番狡黠的编造，用来解释那已为人知的口角与那已为人知的威胁？这故事会不会属实，只是有一处重大的更正——特雷瑟维已将意图付诸实行？它会不会自始至终都是不折不扣的实情？甚或它还越出了实情——此人的念头，其实从未有他自陈的那般黑暗？一连数日，这些问题频频浮上我的心头；但我对它们的真实看法，几乎在离开特雷瑟维的那一刻便已定了。我有违自己的处世原则，不喜欢这个人，对他灵魂的挣扎也出奇地少有同情；然而我不怀疑那挣扎是真实的，也不怀疑他并未犯下这桩罪行。

===Original===
Before Trethewy was brought before the magistrates, a letter arrived which excited my imagination unaccountably, or rather two letters arrived. The day before Vane-Cartwright had left, a letter had arrived for Peters, bearing the postmark of Baghdad. Vane-Cartwright carelessly opened it. He had, I think, at my request, on the day when I was away in London, opened some letters which arrived for Peters' executors. So he had a good excuse for opening this. "Well, that is very uninforming," he said, passing the letter over to me, with an apology for his mistake, and laughing more than was usual with him. Uninforming it certainly was.

"Dear Eustace," it ran, "I am sorry I can tell you nothing about it.—Yours, C. B."

Just a week later, after Vane-Cartwright had left, came another letter from the same place, in the same hand, and almost, but not quite, as brief:

"Dear Eustace, This time I will not delay my answer. Longhurst sailed in the *Eleanor* and she did not go down. To the best of my belief she still sails the seas. I never liked C.—Yours ever, Charles Bryanston."

===Chinese===
在特雷瑟维被解到治安法官面前之前，有一封信寄到了，莫明所以地撩动了我的想象——或者说，是两封信。韦恩-卡特赖特动身离开的前一天，一封写给彼得斯的信寄到，盖着巴格达的邮戳。韦恩-卡特赖特漫不经心地把它拆开了。我想，在我外出去伦敦的那一天，他曾应我的请求，拆阅过几封寄给彼得斯遗嘱执行人的信件。所以拆这一封，他倒有个现成的借口。“唔，这封简直什么都没说。”他说着把信递给我，为自己的失误道了歉，笑得比他惯常的更多。它确实什么都没说。

信上写道：“亲爱的尤斯塔斯：很抱歉，这件事我无可奉告。——你的，C. B.”

整整一个星期之后——其时韦恩-卡特赖特已经走了——又一封信从同一地方寄来，出自同一只手笔，几乎同样简短，只是不尽相同：

“亲爱的尤斯塔斯：这一回我不拖延回信了。朗赫斯特（Longhurst）乘的是‘埃莉诺号’（*Eleanor*），船并没有沉。据我所信，她至今仍航行在海上。我从来就不喜欢 C.——你永远的，查尔斯·布莱恩斯顿（Charles Bryanston）”

## VII

===Original===
After several remands, the proceedings against Trethewy before the magistrates came to a close about the end of February. There was nothing much to note about these proceedings, which ended, as I suppose they must have ended, in his being committed for trial. The reader knows by this time pretty nearly the whole case against him. That Peters had been murdered was certain. The accused had had several altercations with the murdered man. In one of them he had expressed a wish to kill him, and he had repeated this wish to others upon the fatal night. Footprints had been found which, as the reader knows, seemed at first sight plainly indicative of his guilt. Then there was the ladder. It was undoubtedly kept, before the murder, locked up in a place of which only Trethewy had the key. That anyone could have had access to it between the murder and the discovery of the ladder was a view supported only by the uncorroborated statement of the accused that he had left the key of the pump-house that morning, when summoned to speak to the police, and had forgotten to go back for it until the next day. Lastly, the finding of the instrument case, though not very important, at any rate disposed of any improbability that Trethewy would have had such an instrument as the knife that was used.

I daresay this would have been enough to hang a man if this was all; and against this there was nothing to be set, except the immovable persistency of Trethewy and his wife from the first in the tale which they told.

===Chinese===
几度还押之后，特雷瑟维在治安法官面前的那场审理，到二月底前后方才收场。这场审理没有多少值得记下的东西，其结局——我想也只得如此收场——是他被提交巡回法庭受审。到此为止，读者对他的罪状已差不多知道了个全貌。彼得斯死于谋杀，这一点确凿无疑。被告与死者生前曾数度激烈口角。其中一次，他流露过要杀他的念头；而在那致命的一夜，他又把这个念头对旁人重述了一遍。脚印已经找到，而读者知道，那些脚印乍看之下分明指向他的罪行。再有就是那架梯子。案发以前，它无疑锁在一处只有特雷瑟维握有钥匙的地方。至于说有人能在凶案与梯子被发现之间碰过它，此说唯一的凭据，是被告那无人佐证的一面之词：案发那天早晨，他奉召去跟警方问话时，把水泵房的钥匙撂在了原处，直到第二天才想起回去取。最后是那器械匣的查获。此事虽说不要紧，至少也消解了一层疑窦：特雷瑟维手头会有行凶所用的那样一把刀子，这不再是不可能的事了。

倘若事情尽止于此，我想这已足够把一个人送上绞架；而能拿来与之抗衡的，只有一样：特雷瑟维和他的妻子，从最初一刻起，便死死咬定他们那套说法，纹丝不移。

===Original===
Nothing, that is, till after he had been committed for trial. But the very evening after his committal, a slight but almost conclusive circumstance was brought to light, and entirely altered the aspect of the case. That evening I received a visit from Peters’ housemaid, Edith Summers. She had, she said, something on her mind. She had told a falsehood to the police-sergeant on the morning after the murder. She had interrupted the housekeeper to say that the candle by Peters’ bed had been a long candle the night before; she had said this because she had been very severely scolded by the housekeeper for forgetting to put fresh candles in the candlestick; and so she had said what was false, not meaning any harm, but thinking for the moment (as she now tried to explain) that it was true, and that she had done what she had intended. She had confessed to the housekeeper since, but the housekeeper had only said she was an impudent girl to have put in her word then, and had better not put it in again. She had gone to the court expecting to be a witness on some small point and determined to make the matter clear then; but she had not been called. She had spoken to a policeman, and had been told to speak to one of the lawyers. She had tried to get the attention of Trethewy’s lawyer, but he had been too busy to listen to her.

I am ashamed to say that listening to her rather long explanation, I entirely failed to see the significance of what she told me. I said something quite well intentioned about the evil of saying what was not true, and then told the girl kindly, that I did not think there was any harm done. But she had thought about it and was in earnest, and she made me see it in a moment. There were, she explained, other candles in the room, but they were new candles, and they were not lighted that night. From this and what we already knew the conclusion was almost inevitable. Peters was murdered before two inches of ordinary candle, which was burning at 11:30 p.m. on the 28th of January, burnt down.

===Chinese===
我是说，直到他被提交巡回法庭之前，再没有别的东西。可就在他被移交候审的当天傍晚，一桩细微却几乎足以定谳的情节浮出了水面，令全案面貌为之改观。那天傍晚，彼得斯家的女仆伊迪丝·萨默斯登门看我。她说，她心里搁着一件事。凶案次日早晨，她对警佐说了假话。她当时打断了女管家的话头，说彼得斯床头那支蜡烛，头天夜里原是一支长蜡烛；她之所以这么说，是因为忘了往烛台里换上新蜡烛，先前被女管家狠狠训斥了一顿；于是她便说了不实的话——并无半点恶意，只是一时间（她如今竭力这样解释）信以为真，以为自己已把分内的事做妥了。事后她已向女管家认了错，可女管家只说她是个放肆的丫头，当时不该多嘴插话，往后还是少插为妙。开庭那天她去了法庭，指望就某桩小事出庭作证，打定主意届时把这事说个明白；却始终没有人传她。她向一个警察说过这事，人家叫她去找律师谈。她想引特雷瑟维那位律师的注意，可那人忙得根本无暇听她说话。

说来惭愧，听她颠来倒去讲了那么长一篇，我竟全然没有领会她这话里的分量。我还十分好心地发了一通议论，说不实之词是桩罪过，随后又和颜悦色地对姑娘说，依我看，这并没有造成什么损害。可她把这事前前后后想过，态度极其认真；一转眼的工夫，她便让我开了窍。她解释说，房间里另有几支蜡烛，但都是新蜡烛，那天夜里一支也没有点。由这一点，再加上我们已掌握的情况，结论几乎无可回避：彼得斯遇害，是在一支普通蜡烛烧完两英寸之前——那支蜡烛在 1 月 28 日晚上 11:30 还燃着。

---

===Original===
Stupid as it may seem, I had for some time been convinced of Trethewy’s innocence, and yet had never really drawn the necessary inference from it. Of course with the two premises in my mind—Peters was murdered, Trethewy did not murder him—I had been aware, in a sense, of the conclusion, but it had taken no hold of my attention. Now, however, I had evidence of Trethewy’s innocence, which was no longer a private intuition of my own, but was something of which everyone must appreciate the force. Perhaps it was from this, perhaps it was from the sentimental effect of having the time of the crime fixed within such narrow limits; anyhow the thought, “Someone other than Trethewy murdered Peters,” came upon me with a sudden horror which could hardly have been greater if I had only that moment become aware of the original fact of the murder.

I instantly went over in my mind the list of those few who were so placed as to lie within the reach of suspicion. Trethewy could no longer be suspected. Thalberg surely could not. I dismissed the two women servants from my mind immediately. There remained two men—three men—three men, of whom I was one. I knew how easily I could clear myself, for the door had been locked behind me before that candle was lit. But I was the last man known to have seen Peters, and my confused current of thought included me as a man to be suspected. I asked myself of each in turn, is he the guilty man? and in each case I answered no. As I look back now, it seems to me, that the answer “no” did not come to my mind with the same wholehearted conviction in each case. But I did not in the few moments for which I then reflected, I did not till long after do more than go round in this circle: One of us three men murdered Peters. Was it—each of us in turn? No. Could it after all be one of the servants? No! Was there not then in the vast region of possibility some way of accounting for Peters’ death without the guilt of any of us. The plainest reasons bade me answer yes, and yet again I answered no. And so back round the circle.

===Chinese===
这话说出来未免显得愚钝：有一阵子了，我早已确信特雷瑟维是无辜的，却从不曾真正由这份确信推出那个必然的结论。当然，两个前提都摆在我心里——彼得斯被人谋杀了；谋杀他的不是特雷瑟维——在某种意义上，那结论我并非没有意识到，只是它始终不曾攫住我的注意。而如今，我手里有了特雷瑟维无辜的证据；这不再是我一人的私见直觉，而是任谁都必须掂出其分量的事实。这或许是由于前者，或许是由于罪案的时刻被压缩到如此狭窄的界限之内，给人的心头的震动；总之，「谋杀彼得斯的，是特雷瑟维以外的什么人」——这个念头携着一阵骤然的恐怖向我袭来，那恐怖之烈，纵使我直到那一刻才头一回知晓谋杀这桩事实本身，怕也不过如此。

我当即在心中把寥寥几个位置足以落入嫌疑范围的人逐一过了一遍。特雷瑟维已无从怀疑。塔尔贝格想必也不会。两名女仆，我立刻从心里打发了出去。剩下两个男人——三个男人——三个男人，其中一个是我。我知道自己多么容易便可洗刷干净，因为在那支蜡烛点起之前，我身后的门早已锁上。可我是已知最后一个见过彼得斯的人，于是那一团纷乱的思潮，也把我本人卷作了嫌疑的对象。我挨个问自己：他有罪吗？每问一人，我都答：没有。如今回首，我总觉得，那几声「没有」落进心头时，每一回的全然笃定，并不尽同。可在当时思忖的那短短片刻里——乃至此后许久——我所做的，无非是绕着这个圈子打转：我们三个男人中的一个谋杀了彼得斯。是他吗——挨个问过来？不是。到头来难道会是仆人中的一个？不是！那么，在这片广袤的可能性疆域里，难道竟没有一条路，可以解释彼得斯之死，而无须我们中任何一人担罪？最浅白的道理都命我答「有」，可我还是答了「没有」。于是又转回圈子里去。

===Original===
But the girl was with me and I could not keep her waiting forever. I arrested my mental circle where it began, at the thought: it seems Peters was murdered while two inches of ordinary candle, lighted before 11:30 p.m. on the 28th of January, burnt out. I started up to take the girl at once to see the police, but on a sudden idea I desisted. I wrote a note to the housekeeper, asking that the girl should again come to see me at eight in the evening, and I sent a message to the police-sergeant, asking him to come at the same time. Of course I had often interviewed him on parish matters, and having got him settled into the armchair in my study, in which I could usually put him at his ease, I fired upon him the question, “Sergeant, were those tracks, which we found, really there when you came to Mr. Peters’ house in the morning?” Now Sergeant Speke was a very honest man, but he was (most properly, I am sure) a creature of discipline, and his answer threw, for me, a flood of light on the problem how it is that the very best of the police are so ready to back up one another. He answered immediately and with conviction: “Well, you see, sir, it is not for me to judge.” The answer was on the face of it preposterous. He alone had searched the front of the house that morning, and it was for him alone, of all men, to say whether the tracks were there. He obviously did not see this at all, and I was wise enough to let go an opportunity for moralising to him. I beguiled him, with a glass of wine and other devices of the tempter, into feeling himself off duty for the while, and talking with me as fellow-mortal to fellow-mortal. I very soon discovered, first, that Sergeant Speke had searched carefully enough around the house that morning to have seen the tracks if they had been there, and, secondly, that the man, Speke, as distinct from the Sergeant, knew perfectly well that they were not there.

===Chinese===
可姑娘还在我这里等着，我总不能叫她一直等下去。我便把那团兜圈的思绪勒在它的起点上，勒在这个念头上：看来，彼得斯遇害，正落在一支普通蜡烛烧掉两英寸的那段时间里——那支蜡烛在 1 月 28 日晚上 11:30 之前便已点燃。我霍然起身，要立刻带姑娘去见警方，忽生一念，又止住了。我给女管家写了张便条，请她放姑娘晚上八点再来见我；又给警佐捎了口信，请他同一时刻过来。教区的事务上，我自然常同他会面。我把他安顿进书房里那把扶手椅——大凡坐进那把椅子，他多半便能自在下来——随即朝他劈面抛出那个问题：「警佐，我们发现的那些脚印，您那天早晨赶到彼得斯先生宅上时，当真已经在了吗？」斯皮克警佐是个非常诚实的人，但他（我敢说，这再正当不过）是个纪律造出来的生灵；而他这一答，倒替我照亮了一桩悬题：警界里顶顶出色的人物，何以也那般乐于彼此撑腰。他应声而答，斩钉截铁：「呃，您瞧，先生，轮不到我来判断。」这回答摆在明面上是荒唐透顶的。那天早晨，唯独他搜查过宅子的正面；普天之下，也唯独他一个人，说得上脚印在不在那里。他显然全然见不到这一层，而我总算明智，把说教的机会白白放过了。我用一杯酒，外加诱惑者的种种手段，哄得他一时觉得自己脱了差事，同一个凡人对另一个凡人似的同我交谈起来。我很快便查明了两件事：其一，那天早晨，斯皮克警佐在宅子四周搜得够仔细，脚印若真在那里，他不可能看不见；其二，那个作为凡人的斯皮克——有别于作为警佐的斯皮克——心里一清二楚：脚印并不在那里。

===Original===
Not till then did I summon the girl Edith from the servants’ hall where she was waiting. I made her tell her tale. I saw the Sergeant take a due note of it for transmission to those, to me mysterious, headquarters where I supposed all such matters were digested. I got the assurance that Sergeant Speke was really man enough to see that his own evidence, as to the nonexistence of the tracks that morning, would be noted and digested too. I dismissed the Sergeant and Edith, and went slowly to bed. Did I suspect this person? No! Did I suspect that person? N—no. At last I determined that I would not let my suspicions fasten on any one man, while it might be just as reasonable that his suspicions should fasten on me. But my mind remained full of horror and of the image of a candle-end spluttering out, while the man, who had lighted it to read by, lay dead in those bloody sheets. Very, very glad I was that my wife was at last coming home next day.

I suppose it was from the association of two female names that my dreams, when at last I slept, were of nothing more horrible than the ship *Eleanor*, which, as the reader remembers, probably still sailed the seas.

===Chinese===
直到那时，我才把伊迪丝从她候着的下房里唤出来。我让她把事情从头讲了一遍。我看着警佐郑重其事地把它记录下来，好呈送到那些在我看来神秘莫测的上级机关——我猜想，凡属此类事务，都在那里汇总消化。我又从他那里得了保证：斯皮克警佐到底是个够格的汉子，看得清自己那番证词——关于那天早晨脚印并不存在——同样会被记录在案、一并消化。我打发走了警佐和伊迪丝，慢慢地上了床。我怀疑这个人吗？不！我怀疑那个人吗？不——不。最后我拿定主意：绝不让自己的怀疑钉死在哪一个人身上——何况他的怀疑落在我身上，说不定也同样在理。可我的心头依旧塞满恐怖，塞着一幅图景：一段蜡烛头噼噼啪啪地烧到熄灭，而点起它就着读书的那个人，正躺在那血污的被褥里，死了。我实在、实在是满心欢喜——第二天，我的妻子终于要回家了。

我想，多半是两个女人的名字在脑中连到了一处；待我终于入睡，梦里再没有比「埃莉诺号」（*Eleanor*）更可怕的物事——读者当还记得，那条船多半仍航行在海上。

## VIII

===Original===
With some doubt as to whether it was what I ought to do, but with no doubt that it was what I wanted to do, I sought out Callaghan next morning for a final talk with him before he left; for he was at last to tear himself away from the scene which he haunted. I tried on him, I do not know why, the effects of Edith's disclosure without telling him what I now knew about the tracks. I could see that he accepted the truth of the girl's statement, and had grasped, much more quickly than I had, what it imported. It was therefore wearisome to me, and, in my then state of mind, most jarring, that for some time he persisted in playing with the idea that Trethewy might still be guilty. He supported it, as he went on, with more and more far-fetched arguments, so that my patience was nearly at an end, when, to my amazement, I found my friend off at full speed again upon a fresh track, that of Thalberg. I listened, and this time seriously, to several things which he told me about Thalberg, which were new to me and threw an unpleasant light upon him. Then I interposed. Thalberg had left the house with me, and it had been made all but certain that he went straight to his hotel and never left it until many hours after the murder had been discovered. In any case it was not he who had made those tracks, for he had certainly kept in his hotel from early morning on the 29th till he left. And I then told Callaghan my reason for believing that those tracks were made in the middle of the day on the 29th. "My dear friend," he exclaimed, this time with all the appearance of earnestness, "I no more really believe than you do that Thalberg actually did the deed. He is not man enough. But I have a method, I have a method. I am used to these things. I am off to Town now; I shall be there some time; you know my address. I mean," he added grandiloquently, "to work through all the outside circumstances and possibilities of the case, and narrow down gradually to the real heart of the problem; it is my method."

===Chinese===
第二天一早，我去寻卡拉汉，想赶在他动身之前同他作最后一番谈话。该不该这么做，我心里不无犹疑；可我想不想这么做，却半分疑义也没有——因为他总算要把自己从这片他日夜萦绕流连的现场硬生生扯走了。不知为什么，我把伊迪丝披露的情况在他身上试了试分量，却绝口不提我如今对那些脚印的所知。看得出，他认定那姑娘所言属实，而且远比我快地掂出了这话里的干系。因此，他竟一连好些时候死死把玩着「特雷瑟维仍有可能是凶手」的念头不肯撒手，这叫我厌烦，而以我当时的心境，更是刺耳之极。他越往下说，撑持这念头的论据越发不着边际，我的耐性几乎告罄；不料正在此时，令我愕然的是，我这位朋友竟又撒开腿，全速循着一条新的踪迹追了下去——这一回，是塔尔贝格的踪迹。这一回我听得认真了：他把塔尔贝格的几桩事讲给我听，于我都是新闻，而且给此人罩上了一层令人不快的暗影。我便插话了。塔尔贝格那晚是同我一道离开宅子的，而且后来几乎已查得确凿：他径直回了旅馆，直到凶案被发现之后许多个钟头，从未离开过一步。退一万步说，那些脚印也不是他踩出来的，因为29日一清早直到他动身，他分明一直待在旅馆里。接着，我又把我的理由讲给卡拉汉听：为什么我认定那些脚印是29日晌午时分留下的。「亲爱的朋友，」他叫道，这一回满脸郑重其事的神色，「要说塔尔贝格当真下了手，你心里不信，我心里也实在不信。他没那个胆量。可是我有一套方法，我有一套方法。这类事情我是惯了的。我这就上伦敦去，要在那儿盘桓些时日；你知道我的地址。」说到这里他又夸夸其谈起来：「我的意思是，把这案子的外围情形和种种可能一桩桩过一遍，逐步收窄，逼向问题的真正核心——这就是我的方法。」

===Original===
Well, there may have been some method in his madness, for there was certainly some madness in his method. I took leave of him (after he had called, that afternoon, to renew acquaintance with my wife) little foreseeing what his two next steps would be. He stopped on his way to London at the county town, where he went to the county police office to communicate some information or theory about Thalberg. He went on to London, as he had said he would, but, instead of remaining there as he had said, he suddenly departed next day for the Continent, leaving no address behind.

===Chinese===
唉，他的疯癫里或许真藏着几分章法，因为他的章法里确实掺着几分疯癫。当天下午他登门拜访，与我妻子重叙了一回，之后我便与他作别了，几乎预见不到他接下去要走的两步棋。去伦敦的途中，他在郡治停了一停，到郡警察局去通报了关于塔尔贝格的几分情报，或者说几分高见。他如所说的那样继续去了伦敦，却没有如所说的那样留在那里，而是次日突然动身去了欧洲大陆，身后不留一个地址。

===Original===
We have now arrived at the first week in March, the several events (if I may include under the name of events the slow emergence of certain thoughts in my own mind) which prepared the way for the eventual solution of our mystery, occurred at intervals, and in an order of which my memory is not quite distinct, during that and the remaining nine months of the year.

The resolution at which I had arrived, not to occupy my mind with suspicions, or to regard the detection of crime as part of my business, was not a tenable resolution, and it was entirely dissipated by my wife in a talk which we had on the first evening after her arrival. I was aware that she would not be able to share with me in the determination not to harbour suspicions of any particular person, but I had thought she would be averse to my taking positive steps towards the detection of the crime. She, however, was indignant at the idea that I could let things be. "Several innocent men will be under a cloud all their lives," she said, "unless the guilty man is found. There is Trethewy, I suppose they will let him out some day; but who is going to employ him? Not that uncle of his; and we cannot. Who do you suppose is going to see this through if you do not?" She was powerfully seconded in this by a neighbour of ours, now an old man, who had had much experience as a justice. "Mr. Driver," he said, "you may think this is the business of the police, but remember who the police are. They do their ordinary work excellently, but their ordinary work is to deal with ordinary crime. This was not an ordinary crime, and it was done by no ordinary man. If it is ever discovered, it will be by a man whose education gives him a wider horizon than that of professional dealers with criminals."

===Chinese===
我们如今来到了三月的第一个星期。为最终解开我们的谜团铺平道路的那几桩事件（倘若可以容我把某些念头在我自己心里的缓慢浮现也归入事件之列），是在那一周以及这一年内余下的九个月里，间隔着先后发生的；至于先后次序，我的记忆已不十分分明。

我先前立定的决心——不以疑虑占据心神，不把侦缉罪案看作自己的本分——原是撑不住的；她到家后的头一个晚上，我们谈了一席话，这决心便叫她化解得干干净净。我原就知道，要她同我一起抱定不猜疑任何特定个人的决心，她是办不到的；但我以为，对我着手采取积极步骤去侦破此案，她总会不以为然。谁知一想到我竟可以听之任之，她便愤愤然。「真凶一天不找到，就有好几个清白人一辈子顶着乌云，」她说，「就说特雷瑟维吧，我料他们总有一天会放他出来；可往后谁肯雇他？他那位叔叔是不会雇的；我们也雇不了。你若不管，你倒说说，还能指望谁把这桩事管到底？」我们的一位邻居给了她强有力的支持——此公如今已是老人，做治安法官时阅历极深。「德赖弗（Driver）先生，」他说，「你也许以为这是警察的分内事；可你得想想警察是些什么人。日常的差事，他们办得极为出色，但他们的日常差事，对付的是寻常罪案。这桩案子不是寻常罪案，干下它的也绝非寻常之人。这案子倘若终有侦破的一天，破案者必是那样一个人：他所受的教育，给了他比那些以同罪犯打交道为业者更宽的眼界。」

===Original===
I do not know how far the reader may have been inclined to suspect Callaghan (that depends, I suppose, on whether the reader has been able to form any idea of his character, and I myself had not, so far, formed any coherent idea of his character; there seemed little coherence in it), but the police certainly had begun to suspect him.

On a superficial view of the matter there was every reason to do so. Short of bolting on the night of the murder, before it was discovered, he had done all that, theoretically, a guilty man should have done. He had lost no time whatever in attempting to put suspicion on one innocent man. He had striven to intermeddle officiously in the investigations conducted by the police. There was more than one apparent lie in the information he had given. He had haunted the scene of the crime as though it fascinated him. When the first innocent man was cleared, he had at once suggested another man, who was almost certainly innocent also, and he had then, after giving false accounts of his intentions, quitted the country without leaving his address. Then he was certainly in the house when the crime was committed. His movements on the following day were nearly accounted for, but not so fully that he could not have made those false tracks. After all it was a circumstance of deep suspicion that he had been so quick to recognise the peculiar print of Trethewy's boot.

===Chinese===
读者到了这一步，究竟在多大程度上倾向于怀疑卡拉汉，我不得而知（我想，这取决于读者能否对他的为人形成一点概念；而我自己呢，直到那时也还没能对他的为人形成任何连贯的概念——他那个人身上，本来就少有连贯可言），但警察确凿已经起意怀疑他了。

从表面上看，他们有十足的理由这样做。除了在案发当夜、罪行尚未败露之前畏罪潜逃这一条，凡是理论上一个有罪之人该做的事，他一件不落全做了。他一星半点工夫也没耽误，就试图把嫌疑栽到一个无辜者头上。他千方百计要越俎代庖，插手警方主持的侦查。他提供的供述里，明摆着的谎话不止一处。他流连于罪案现场不去，仿佛那里对他施着魔咒。头一个无辜者洗脱之后，他立即又指认了另一个人——那人几乎可以断定同样无辜；随后，在对自己的去向作了一番不实的交代之后，他不辞而别，离开了英国，不留地址。再者，案发之时，他确实就在那座宅子里。案发次日，他的行踪差不多都有了下落，却又没有严丝合缝到让他绝无可能去伪造那些假脚印。说到底，特雷瑟维那只靴子印痕独特，他却一眼认了出来，这本身就是一重极深的疑点。

===Original===
Alas, even to the test cui bono, "that stock question of Cassius, 'whom did it profit?'" Callaghan responded ill. I knew, and somewhat later in reply to an enquiry by the police, it was my duty to say, that Callaghan was in a certain sense a gainer by Peters' death. He had been a most imprudent investor (not, I believe, a speculator), and had in his embarrassment borrowed £2,000 from Peters. Peters, while living, would not have been at all hard on him if he had been honestly unable to pay, but was just the man to have made Callaghan's life a burden to him if he thought he was not doing his best to keep above water. Peters' will cancelled the debt, and it was not impossible that Callaghan knew it. But this last point illustrates the real weakness of the argument against him. Nobody could know Callaghan a little and think that either this interest in the will or any other point in this hypothetical story of his crime, however much it might be like human nature, was in the least like him.

===Chinese===
唉，就连 cui bono（于谁有利）的检验——用卡西乌斯（Cassius）那句惯用的老话来问：「这对谁有好处？」——卡拉汉也交不出好答卷。我知道——而且不久之后，在答复警方问询时，我有责任说出——卡拉汉在某种意义上是彼得斯之死的受益人。他投资向来极不审慎（我相信倒算不上投机），窘迫之际向彼得斯借了两千英镑。彼得斯在世时，他若确乎无力偿还，彼得斯绝不会苛刻相逼；可他若被认为没有竭力撑住水面，彼得斯却偏偏是那种会叫卡拉汉的日子变成苦役的人。彼得斯的遗嘱勾销了这笔债务，而卡拉汉未必不知这一点。然而恰恰是这最后一层，显出了对他不利的指控真正的软肋。但凡对卡拉汉略有几分认识的人，都不会觉得——无论是对遗嘱这笔利益的指望也好，还是这套假想罪案故事里的任何一环也好，纵然再合乎普通人性——有半分像他本人。

===Original===
Here, for want of a good description of him, are a few traits of his sojourn in my parish. He was, it is true, with difficulty dragged out of a furious brawl with a gentleman from the North of Ireland who, he said, had blasphemed against the Pope. The man had not so blasphemed, and Callaghan himself was not a Roman Catholic. On the other hand, he had habitually since his arrival lain in wait for the school children to give them goodies and so forth. He assaulted and thrashed two most formidable ruffians who were maltreating a horse, and then plastered their really horrible bruises with so much blarney that they forgave, not merely him, but the horse. He had brought for Peters, with infinite pride, a contraband cargo of his native potheen, a terrible fluid; and after Peters' death he would sit up alone in that desolate house, drinking, not the potheen, which, in intended charity, he suggested that I should bestow on the poor in the workhouse, but Mrs. Travers' barley water, and writing a rather good and entirely bright and innocent fairy story.

===Chinese===
这里既然拿不出一份对他的出色描写，就记下他客居我教区期间的几件小事吧。不错，有一回他同一位北爱尔兰来的绅士咆哮着扭作一团，人们好不容易才把他俩扯开——照他的说法，那人出言亵渎了教皇。那人其实并没有这样亵渎，而卡拉汉自己也并非天主教徒。而另一面，自打来到本地，他惯常半路设伏，守着放学的孩子，好给他们发糖果点心之类。他还动粗痛揍过两个凶悍骇人的恶徒，只因他们虐待一匹马；揍完之后，又拿成堆的花言巧语敷在他们那真正骇人的青肿上，哄得两人非但不记恨他，连那匹马也一并饶恕了。他曾怀着无上的自豪，为彼得斯运来一整批走私的他家乡的私酿烈酒（potheen）——一种可怕的液体；彼得斯死后，他常独自在那座凄凉的宅子里坐到深夜，喝的却不是那私酿酒——他心存善念，建议我把酒布施给济贫院里的穷人——而是特拉弗斯太太的大麦水，一面还写着一篇相当不坏、通体明快而无邪的童话。

===Original===
This is emphatically not evidence, but it made me sure of Callaghan's innocence. Looking at what I suppose was evidence, I had wondered whether I was not soft in this, and I brought the matter to the test of my wife's judgment. I knew that, at least at her earlier meetings with Callaghan, she had disliked him, and, out of the facts which she knew already, I made what I flattered myself was a very telling case against him. It did not disconcert me that the lady, who, when told of his flight, had trusted he would remain out of England till she went abroad, said without much interest, "What stuff," and then suddenly kindling, exclaimed, "What, Robert, are you turning against that poor man?" When I asked for the reasons why she scouted the idea of his guilt, she seemed to consider the request quite frivolous; but at last I extracted from her a sentence which expressed what I think was at the root of my own thought. "Mr. Callaghan," she said, "is violent enough to commit a murder and cunning enough to conceal anything, but I cannot imagine his violence and his cunning ever working together."

Of course we both thought of him as sane, though he was just one of those people to whose doings one constantly applies the epithet "mad."

===Chinese===
这些分明算不得证据，却叫我确信卡拉汉无辜。而对着那些我姑且算作证据的东西，我又暗暗疑心自己在这一点上是否心肠太软，于是把这件事放到我妻子的判断跟前，让它受一受检验。我知道，至少在早先与卡拉汉见面时，她是不喜欢他的；凭着一些她已知晓的事实，我拼出一套指证他的说辞，自以为极有分量。这位夫人先前听说他出走时，还指望他能待在英国境外，直到她动身出国为止；此刻她意兴阑珊地丢下一句「什么昏话」，倒没叫我狼狈；可紧接着她忽然来了火气，叫道：「怎么，罗伯特（Robert），你也要同那个可怜人作对了？」我问她凭什么对卡拉汉有罪之说嗤之以鼻，她似乎觉得这请求无聊透顶；但最后我还是从她口中逼出了一句话，而这句话，我想，正道出了我自己思绪深处的根由。「卡拉汉先生这个人，」她说，「论凶暴，足够他去杀一个人；论狡诈，足够他把什么都瞒住；可我无法想象，他的凶暴与他的狡诈竟能同心协力。」

当然，我们俩都当他神智健全，尽管他恰恰属于那一类人：对他们的行径，人们总是一而再、再而三地冠以「疯」字。

## IX

===Original===
The enquiry upon which I had now stirred myself to enter, could not be an easy one, but it should have seemed for the present to be narrowed down to a question about a single man. Perhaps it was from repugnance against consciously going about to hang a man who had sheltered under my roof, that I did not even then definitely put to myself the question of that man’s guilt. By some half-conscious sequence of thought I was led to begin my search far afield. It started with the two letters which had come for Peters from Mr. Charles Bryanston, or rather first with the later letter.

I had some time before written briefly and formally to Mr. Bryanston to acquaint him with the fact of Peters’ murder, but had, for a while since, thought no more of him. Now I began to do what one very seldom does, steadily and methodically think. I mooned up and down with a pipe in my garden or in the lanes. I sat, with those letters in my hand, alone before the fire. I sat at my writing-table with paper before me, and made incoherent jottings with a pencil. I should be afraid to say how often and how long I did all these seemingly idle things. Till at last, in the time between tea and dinner, with the children playing in the room, I arrived at actually spelling the matter out.

===Chinese===
我如今强打精神着手进行的这项查究，注定不会轻松；不过就目前而论，它似乎本已收窄为关于单独一个人的问题。或许正是因为不忍存心奔走、亲手把一个曾在我屋檐下栖身的人送上绞架，直到那时，我也没有向自己明确摆出此人罪责的问题。循着某种半明半昧的思绪，我竟被引到远处去寻起线索来。入手处，便是查尔斯·布莱恩斯顿先生寄给彼得斯的那两封信——或者不如说，先从后寄到的那一封开始。

此前我曾简短而拘礼地致函布莱恩斯顿先生，把彼得斯遇害一事相告，此后一度没有再想起这个人。如今我开始做一件人们极难得做的事：沉下心来，有条有理地思考。我叼着烟斗，在花园里、在村巷间失魂落魄地踱来踱去。我把那两封信攥在手里，独自坐在炉火前。我坐在写字台前，面前摊着纸，用铅笔涂下些前言不搭后语的札记。这些看似闲散无谓的功课，我做了多少回、耗了多少时辰，我真不敢说。直到最后，在午后茶点与晚饭之间那段时间，孩子们还在屋里嬉戏，我终于把这桩事情真正一字一字拼解明白了。

===Original===
“This time I will not delay my answer.” “This time﻿—” Then at other times he did delay his answer. That might have some significance when I turned to the earlier letter. “This time I will not delay my answer.” It was an answer to a question in a letter just received from Peters, an answer probably by return of post. Why not delay it this time as usual? Why, of course, because the question was one which both to Peters and to Bryanston seemed important, perhaps momentous. Simple enough so far. “Longhurst did sail in the *Eleanor*, and she did not go down.” It was clear enough that someone had thought that Longhurst had sailed in a ship that did go down. Peters had thought otherwise, and Peters was right. What of that? There is nothing momentous in that. Stop, though. It is not necessarily that. Someone need not have thought it﻿—he may have said it to Peters, and Peters may have thought it was a lie. And what did it matter, and why did someone say it? Well, of course, Longhurst would be dead if the ship had gone down; and Longhurst was not really dead, and someone was interested in saying that he was. Perhaps Longhurst was the next heir to some property, and search ought to have been made for him; and my mind wandered over all the stories I had ever read of lost heirs, in fact or in fiction. Or perhaps﻿—Who said Longhurst and his ship went down? “C.” said it, whoever “C.” might be.

===Chinese===
「这一回我不会耽搁回信了。」「这一回——」那么，其余那些时候，他确是耽搁了回信的。这层意思，等我转到早先那封信时，或许自有其分量。「这一回我不会耽搁回信了。」这是对彼得斯刚寄到的那封信里某个问题的答复，多半还是趁下一班邮班当即回的。这一回为什么不照例耽搁？哦，当然是因为，那个问题在彼得斯与布莱恩斯顿两人看来都非同小可，说不定事关重大。至此为止，都简单得很。「朗赫斯特确实搭乘埃莉诺号出了海，而那船并没有沉。」显而易见，有人曾以为朗赫斯特乘的那艘船沉了。彼得斯不作此想，而彼得斯是对的。这又怎样？这里头谈不上什么事关重大。且慢，未必是那样。那人也未必真的作此想——他可能只是对彼得斯这么说过，而彼得斯可能认定那是句谎话。这又有什么要紧？那人为什么要那样说？嗯，当然啦，船若沉了，朗赫斯特便是死了；朗赫斯特其实没死，偏有人巴不得说他死了。说不定朗赫斯特是某宗产业的下一顺位继承人，本该着人寻访他的下落；我的思绪于是飘过了平生读过的种种失踪继承人的故事，无论实录还是虚构。再不然——是谁说朗赫斯特连人带船沉了海？是「C.」说的，管他这「C.」究竟是谁。

===Original===
Then I took up the earlier letter. I knew from the other letter that this had been sent late. There was nothing further to be gained from the words of it, but a flood of suspicion broke upon me as I held it in my hand. Had “C.” another initial to his surname, a double name? Did I know this “C.”? Had I not seen this very letter in the hands of “C.”? Had I not thought it rather odd that he, a man so decidedly “all there,” had opened and read it before it was given to me? Had I not rather wondered at the pains he had kindly taken to help me with several letters before? Did he not laugh rather strangely as he read it, though I never heard him laugh at anything amusing? Did he not go away just after the letter came, though he had not been intending to go so soon? Was it conceivable that he knew that Peters had asked that question, and thought the first letter (“very uninforming,” as he called it) was the answer to that question, and an answer which made him safe? After that one laugh I thought he became suddenly downcast. Had he really read in that letter that he need not have feared Peters, and that he had﻿—yes, murdered him for nothing? Had the accident that Peters had written, perhaps long before, some unimportant question to Bryanston, and the accident that Bryanston had delayed his answer betrayed this man into leaving me alone with my letters a week too soon; and would this trifling mistake lead him to﻿—to the gallows? and I remembered with a start the grim end which I was preparing. Yes, all this was conceivable. There is an old maxim that you should beware of going back upon your first instinctive impressions of liking or dislike when you happen to have them. There are qualifications to it; the repulsions that start from ugliness or strangeness or difference of opinion may not be safe guides. But broadly the maxim is true. It was true in this instance. No, I too had never liked “C.”

===Chinese===
随后我拿起早先那封信。从另一封信里我已得知，这一封当初寄出得很迟。信上的字句再榨不出更多的东西，可我把它捏在手里，一股怀疑的洪流当头浇下。「C.」的姓氏里，是不是还另有一个首字母，是个复姓？我认得这个「C.」么？我难道不曾亲眼看见，正是这封信捏在「C.」的手里？他——一个头脑那么分明「灵光」的人——竟在信交到我手上之前先拆开读过，我难道不曾觉得此事颇为蹊跷？此前他好心费神替我料理过好几封信，我难道不曾暗自称奇？他读这封信时难道不是笑得相当古怪么——尽管我从不曾听见他对任何有趣之事发笑？信刚到不久他岂不就动身走了么——尽管他原本并无意走得那么急？可不可能，他知道彼得斯问过那个问题，并以为头一封信——他称之为「言之无物」的那封——正是对那个问题的答复，而且是一封足以保他平安的答复？我记得，他单单笑了那一声之后，人便骤然委顿下去。他是否当真从那封信里读出了：他本不必惧怕彼得斯，而他竟——是啊——白白谋害了此人？莫非是两桩偶然——彼得斯或许早在此前许久，给布莱恩斯顿写信问过某个无关紧要的问题；布莱恩斯顿又耽搁了回信——诱使此人早了一周，撇下我和我的信件独自离去？而这桩细枝末节的差错，会不会把他引向——引向绞架？我不禁悚然一惊，想起自己正在筹办的，是怎样一个阴惨的结局。是的，这一切都讲得通。有句古老的箴言：你对一个人起了好恶的直觉印象——若碰巧起了——切莫轻易将它推翻。这话自有分寸的限制：那些由丑陋、怪异或意见相左而生的反感，未必是可靠的向导。但大体而言，这句箴言是对的。在这一桩事上，它也是对的。不错，我本来也从来不曾喜欢过「C.」。

===Original===
It is strange that I should have received Mr. Bryanston’s answer the very next morning, a long, full, warmhearted letter on the death of the friend to whose letters in life﻿—and what letters Peters wrote!﻿—he made such scrappy replies. In a P.S. at the end, as if the writer had hesitated whether to write it, were the words: “It is curious and may be news to you that Mr. Peters, at the time he was murdered, was unravelling the mystery of another murder, committed, as he suspected, many years ago.”

So then, as I had half-guessed, Longhurst was dead. It was not that he was alive and Cartwright pretended he was dead, he was dead, and Cartwright had a motive for falsely pretending he was drowned.

===Chinese===
说来也怪，恰恰在第二天早晨，我便收到了布莱恩斯顿先生的回信——一封悼念亡友的长信，写得情辞恳切、备极周详；而对这位朋友生前的来信——彼得斯写的是些什么样的信啊！——他素来只回以零碎草草的短简。信末有一则附言，落笔的人仿佛踌躇过该不该写：「说来奇怪，或许也是您未曾听说的：彼得斯先生遇害之时，正在解开另一桩谋杀案的疑团；据他猜想，那桩案子是许多年前犯下的。」

于是，正如我隐约猜中的那样，朗赫斯特死了。并非他人还活着、由卡特赖特谎称他已死——他是死了，而卡特赖特自有其动机，才要假称他溺毙身亡。

## X

===Original===
“*Tout comprendre, c'est tout pardonner*,” is, I do not doubt, a saying which has its truth. Nevertheless, I have generally noticed, when I have read much about murders or other great crimes, or about the social or political misdeeds which are not called crimes, that every piece of additional knowledge about the manner in which the thing was done, the inducements that led to it, the conduct that followed it, has, for me at least, set the capital act of wrong in a more hideous light. It is not, I think, that the picturesque circumstances, like the guttering candle whose image got on my nerves that night, affect me profoundly. It is, I believe, that, while many men, most if you like, are middling, the distinctly bad are really much worse and the distinctly good are really much better than the world of middling people is at all ready to allow. When I looked at the whole circumstances of the crime, as I now conceived them, a great hatred of Vane-Cartwright possessed my soul. There was a passage in my subsequent course with regard to him, when a reason personal to myself had just been added to the cause of my hate, upon which I look back sometimes with self-disgust, but I cannot think that the desire, which first prompted me to fasten myself upon Vane-Cartwright and try to drag him down, was an impure desire, or that it consorted ill with the inner meaning of those precepts which it was my profession to teach.

Whether it was right or wrong, the strength of the feeling which then animated me showed itself in my resolve to think calmly and to act circumspectly. I was conscious that the structure of my theory was held together by no firm rivets of verifiable fact, but by something which must be called feeling. I did not distrust my theory on that account; but I did distrust myself, and I determined, in what lay before me, to take as few impulsive steps and to draw as few impulsive conclusions as I could.

===Chinese===
“理解一切，便是宽恕一切”（*Tout comprendre, c'est tout pardonner*）——这话自有其道理，我并不怀疑。然而，每逢读到许多关于谋杀或其他重大罪行的记载，或是那些不被称为罪行的社会或政治恶行，我大体总会注意到：关于事情如何做成、由何种诱因所致、其后行止又如何，每多知道一分，至少在我这里，那桩首要的恶行便显得愈发狰狞。我想，深深打动我的并非那些情景鲜明的细节，比如那晚搅得我心神不宁的、那支摇曳将熄的蜡烛的意象。我相信，真正的原因在于：许多人——你若愿意，可以说大多数人——都属平庸之辈，而昭彰的恶人实在远比平庸众人组成的世界所肯承认的更坏，昭彰的善人也实在远比它所肯承认的更好。当我依照如今心中的构想、审视这桩罪行的全部情形时，一股对韦恩-卡特赖特的深仇大恨攫住了我的灵魂。此后我在对待他的过程中，有一段经历——那时恰有一桩与我自身攸关的理由，刚刚加进了我仇恨的缘由之中——如今回想起来，我有时仍不免自我嫌恶；但我不能认为，最初促使我咬住韦恩-卡特赖特、竭力要把他拖下马来的是一种不纯的欲望，也不能认为它与那些戒律的内在意涵有什么扞格——传授那些戒律，本是我的天职。

这感觉或对或错，它当时激励于我的那股力量，正显现在我冷静思考、审慎行事的决心之中。我意识到，我这套理论的结构，并非由可验证事实的坚固铆钉铆合而成，而是由一种只能称之为感觉的东西维系在一起的。我并不因此便怀疑我的理论；但我确实怀疑我自己，于是我下定决心，在今后面临的一切之中，尽可能少迈冲动的步子，尽可能少下冲动的断语。

===Original===
Reflecting, next morning, on what could be done immediately to bring my hypothesis to the test of fact, I looked in the *Postal Guide* for such information as it gave about the mails to and from Baghdad. I also verified my impression as to the date of that occasion when Vane-Cartwright, staying at the hotel, had spent the evening with Peters. From what I found it seemed to me that a letter to Baghdad, posted that night, might have been expected to bring an answer back by the date on which the first letter from Bryanston came to my hands, or even a few days earlier, but that the delays of steamers might easily bring it about that an answer should not arrive till a week later, that is, when the second letter from Bryanston came to me. So far then there was nothing to make my conclusion impossible. I may add here that the enquiries which I made, as soon as I saw how to do it, confirmed what I gathered from the *Postal Guide*, and showed that on this occasion such a delay of the mails had actually happened.

But, assuming this about the mails, what a frail edifice my theory still remained! Upon most careful reconsideration, I saw, as the reader may see, that it fitted in easily with all the known facts. It was just as well founded as many things which are taught as established truths of science or history. But as for expecting the law to hang Vane-Cartwright upon this, I myself, fantastically no doubt, refrained a little later from blackballing him at a distinguished club, of which, oddly enough, I had in my ambitious youth become a member. In large part the case, so to call it, against him rested on my observations of his demeanour in my house, and especially of his conduct in regard to my business as executor and my letters. This was precise and cogent enough for me, the observer at first hand; but it was too much matter of general impression to be of use to anyone but me. Then the attribution of that early murder to Vane-Cartwright seemed to me absolutely requisite to make his murder of Peters conceivable. But it was the work of my imagination. In the region of palpable facts, one thing alone was evidence against Vane-Cartwright and not against any other man. It will be remembered that, when Callaghan first denounced Trethewy, Vane-Cartwright said that Trethewy’s behaviour in his presence to Peters had been friendly and respectful. He knew, I now told myself, a better way than expressing suspicion of Trethewy, and while by his stealthy act he fabricated evidence against him, he contrived by his words to cast on Callaghan alone the risk of thereafter appearing as an innocent man's traducer. But his cunning had made a slip. It was gratuitous in doing so to have uttered a refutable lie as to Trethewy’s conduct in his presence. He was not the man to have seen the imprudence of this. It would have been to him inconceivable that Trethewy should confess the full extent of his wrong conduct to me. And so, not from any want of coolness, he had provided me with the one scrap of ordinary evidence necessary to give firmness to that belief of mine which might otherwise have seemed a mere bubble.

===Chinese===
第二天早晨，我思量着眼下能立即做些什么，好让我的假设经受事实的检验，便翻开《邮政指南》（*Postal Guide*），查找其中所载的巴格达往来邮件信息。我还核实了自己对那个日期的印象，即韦恩-卡特赖特下榻旅馆期间与彼得斯共度晚间的那个晚上。从查到的结果看，依我之见，当晚寄出的一封发往巴格达的信，按理本可指望在布莱恩斯顿的第一封来信送到我手里之日、甚至提早几天便收到回音；但轮船航期的延误也很容易造成这样的结果：回信迟至一周之后方才抵达——也就是布莱恩斯顿的第二封来信寄到我手中之时。由此可见，至少到这一步，尚无任何东西足以使我的结论归于不可能。我不妨在此补上一句：后来我一弄清门道便立即着手进行的那些查询，证实了我从《邮政指南》中得来的信息，并表明那一回邮件确曾发生过这样的延误。

然而，即便把邮件这一节假定为真，我的理论仍是一座何其单薄的建筑物！经过最审慎的重新考虑，我看出——读者或许也能看出——它与一切已知事实都能轻易吻合。它的根基，与许多被当作科学或历史的既定真理传授的东西同样牢靠。但至于指望法律凭这一点把韦恩-卡特赖特送上绞架——且说一桩无疑匪夷所思的事：不久之后，在一家显贵的俱乐部里，我本人竟没有投黑球反对他入会；说来也怪，我雄心勃勃的青年时代还曾是那家俱乐部的会员。这个姑且称之为案件的东西，其中不利于他的部分，大体建立在我对他在我家中言谈举止的观察之上，尤其是他在涉及我遗嘱执行人职责的事务以及我的信件上的行止。对身为第一手观察者的我来说，这足够精确、足够有力；但它过多属于总体印象一类，除我之外，于任何人都派不上用场。再者，在我看来，唯有把那桩早先的谋杀归到韦恩-卡特赖特头上，他之谋杀彼得斯才变得可以理解。但那是我想象力的产物。在确凿事实的领域内，只有一件事是指向韦恩-卡特赖特而不指向任何旁人的证据。人们当还记得，卡拉汉第一次指控特雷瑟维时，韦恩-卡特赖特曾说过，特雷瑟维当着他的面对彼得斯的态度素来友善而恭敬。我如今对自己说，他深谙一条比表露对特雷瑟维的猜疑更好的路子：一面以鬼祟之举为特雷瑟维罗织罪证，一面又以言辞设局，让卡拉汉独自承担日后再被视为诬陷无辜者的风险。但他的狡诈失了一着。此中他本不必多此一举地说下那句可以驳倒的谎——关于特雷瑟维当着他的面如何行事的那句。他本就不是那种看得出此举有欠审慎的人。在他看来，特雷瑟维竟会向我招认自己恶行的全部底细，这根本不可想象。于是——并非因为欠了冷静——他亲手供给了我唯一的一星寻常证据，足以让我那个否则便不过是一场泡影的信念有了坚实的支撑。

## XI

===Original===
By the time that my wife, who had been again obliged to be in London while I was spelling out this story, had returned, I had long come to the conclusion that my theory had enough in it to be worth submitting to her criticism. But she forestalled me with news of her own, and news which concerned Vane-Cartwright. The young lady, Miss Denison, whom he was to have married had suddenly broken off the engagement within two days of his joining her upon the Riviera. The girl could give no good reason for her conduct, and her own people loudly condemned it; they had been against the engagement, for the difference of age was too great; they were still more against the flighty breach of it; but she was obdurate. She and her people returned home for Easter, and my wife, who had already known her a little, now met her several times at the house of a common friend in London. The foolish or unhappy young lady had given my wife her confidence. Far from having any suspicion about the murder, she had never even heard, when she made her decision, that there had been a murder at all; for she and her mother did not read news of that order, and Vane-Cartwright, though he had said that he had been through a dreadful experience, of which he was anxious to tell her, had not yet said what it was. There had evidently been a quite unaccountable quarrel in which the high-tempered girl had, in all things external, begun, continued and ended in the wrong; and she did not now defend herself. Somehow, she said, he was changed. No, not in his manner to her; she had not doubted his attachment to her. Only she had thought she had loved him before, and she knew now that she did not. Something, which she had seen in him before but not disliked, now jarred upon her feelings in a new way. She had been very, very foolish, very, very wrong; she could explain nothing; she was very unhappy, very angry with herself; but this she knew, and this alone she knew, that it would be wrong for her to become William Vane-Cartwright’s wife.

So much my wife told me. Then, with that precipitancy in travelling to remote conclusions which sometimes seems so perilous in able women, she said, as quietly as if it were the most obvious comment, “Robert, it was Vane-Cartwright that did the murder.” Now she had never even spoken to him.

===Chinese===
当我在一页页拼凑这个故事的时候，我妻子又因事不得不去了伦敦；待她归来，我早已得出结论：我那套推论已有足够的分量，值得交给她评一评了。不料她先声夺人，带来了她自己的消息，而这消息恰恰关乎韦恩-卡特赖特。他原定要娶的那位年轻小姐——丹尼森小姐——在他到里维埃拉与她相聚不出两天，竟突然解除了婚约。姑娘对自己的举动说不出个像样的理由，她的家人对之大加责难；当初订婚他们就不赞成，因为两人年龄悬殊太大；如今这桩轻率的毁约，他们就更不能容了；可她执拗得很，寸步不让。复活节时，她随家人回了英国；我妻子与她原本有些泛泛之交，这时又在伦敦一位共同朋友的宅邸与她屡屡相见。这位不知该说痴、还是该说不幸的年轻小姐，向我妻子倾吐了心里话。她何曾对那桩谋杀起过半点疑心；直到拿定主意的那一刻，她压根儿没听说过世上有过这么一桩谋杀；因为她和母亲从不读那一类的新闻，而韦恩-卡特赖特虽然说过自己经历了一场可怕的事、急着要讲给她听，却至今没讲出那是怎么一回事。两人之间显然起过一场全然无从解释的口角；就外表的种种而论，这位烈性子的姑娘起头错在她、中间错在她、收梢还错在她；而她如今也并不为自己辩白。她说，不知怎么，他变了。不，不是他待她的态度变了；他对她的情意，她不曾有过怀疑。只是从前她以为自己爱他，如今知道自己并不爱。他身上有某种东西，她从前也见过，并不讨厌，如今却以一种全新的方式硌得她心里难受。她说自己太傻太傻了、错得太深太深了；她什么也解释不出来；她心里很不痛快，狠狠地怨着自己；可有一样她知道，而且唯有这一样她知道得笃定：去做威廉·韦恩-卡特赖特的妻子，是不对的。

我妻子对我讲的就是这些。随后，她带着那种直奔遥远结论的急切——这种急切，在能干的女人身上有时显得颇为危险——用一种仿佛说着天下最明白不过的话的平静语气开了口："罗伯特，杀人的就是韦恩-卡特赖特。"须知她与此人素未谋面，连一句话也不曾交谈过。

===Original===
Accordingly, she received my theory of the murder almost with enthusiasm. None the less, she immediately put her finger upon the weakest part of it. “I wonder, all the same,” she exclaimed, “why he murdered Eustace?” “Why,” I said, “he saw in a moment that Eustace knew he was lying and suspected him of the murder.” “That would not have been enough,” she said; “he must be a very cool-headed man from the way he behaved after the murder, and he would never have run the risk he ran by a second murder, if there had not been much more than suspicion of the first.” “Then,” I suggested, “perhaps Eustace already knew it, and the lie he told only provoked Eustace into showing it.” “If,” she replied, “Eustace knew it, he would never have had him within his doors.” “Well,” I said wearily, because I could not immediately see how to answer, “perhaps he did not murder Eustace.” Then she turned on me with a woman’s promptitude and a woman’s injustice: “You can always argue me down,” she said, “but he did murder Eustace Peters, and you have got to find out all about it and bring him to justice. I am sure you have the ability to do it. You may have to wait, but, if you wait patiently and keep your eyes open, all sorts of things will turn up to help you. I shall be very angry with you,” she added, in a tone not at all suggestive of anger, “if you do not do it.”

===Chinese===
于是，对于我那套破案推论，她几乎是怀着热忱接受了。尽管如此，她还是立刻点中了其中最薄弱的一环。"只是我始终不明白，"她扬声道，"他为什么要杀尤斯塔斯？""这还不明白，"我说，"他一眼便看出尤斯塔斯已经识破他在撒谎，疑心到了他头上。""光凭这个还不够，"她说，"从他在凶案之后的举止来看，他准是个头脑极冷静的人；倘若第一桩案子上他所背的不止是区区嫌疑，他是绝不会为着第二桩凶案去冒他所冒的那份险的。""那么，"我试探着说，"也许尤斯塔斯本已知情，他撒的那个谎，不过是撩得尤斯塔斯把这知情露了出来。""倘若尤斯塔斯早已知情，"她答道，"那就绝不会放他进自家门了。""好吧，"我疲惫地说，因为一时想不出怎样应对，"也许他并没有杀尤斯塔斯。"这时她带着女人的爽利、也带着女人的不讲理，向我转过身来："你总能在口头上驳倒我，"她说，"可杀害尤斯塔斯·彼得斯的确实是他，你必须查个水落石出，把他绳之以法。我确信你有这份本事。你也许得等，但只要有耐心地等、睁大眼睛留神，各式各样的事情自会冒出来帮你的忙。你要是不办成这件事，"她又补了一句，用的口吻里丝毫没有动怒的意思，"我可是要大为光火的。"

===Original===
I felt, like my wife, that it was a matter of waiting for what would turn up. In the neighbourhood of the murder there was probably very little to turn up. The police, I felt no doubt, had made all manner of enquiries; and as for anything that I was likely to pick up, I supposed I had already heard all, and more than all that any person in the neighbourhood knew about the matter. I may anticipate a little and say that in the whole of the four months, which, as it proved, were all that remained to me as Rector of Long Wilton, no fresh information was given to me by my neighbours there. It soon occurred to me that the murder of Longhurst, far away and long ago, might be easier to trace than the recent, but perhaps more carefully veiled, crime committed to cover it. Peters, I reasoned, must have been in possession of proofs of it, and probably, as I searched his voluminous papers, something would appear to indicate the nature of those proofs. I began, as in any case I should have done, a careful reading of his papers. It took up no small part of my spare time, for I found that he had prepared little enough for immediate publication, but fuller and more valuable materials for his projected book of psychology than I should at all have expected from his manner of proceeding. But, of what now interested me more than my friend’s philosophy, I found nothing in all this mass of letters and notes and journals; nothing, that is, which threw direct light on this mystery, for indeed his psychological notes and my discussions with a friend of his, an Oxford philosophy tutor, to whom I eventually committed them, did, I think, influence me not a little in one important part of my enquiry later.

In pushing enquiries further afield there was need for some caution. An indiscretion might have brought what I was doing unnecessarily soon to the notice of the suspected man, and the great ability with which I credited him might suggest some effective scheme for baffling my search. But of course I wrote early to Mr. Bryanston to ask if he would tell me to whom he alluded as “C.,” whether Longhurst was the man whom Peters suspected had been murdered, and whether I inferred rightly that “C.” was involved in this suspicion.

===Chinese===
同我妻子一样，我也觉得这桩事全在等着有什么自行冒头。在凶案发生地左近，多半冒不出什么来。警方的种种查访，我相信早已做得面面俱到；至于我自己可能拾获的东西，本地区任何人所知道的，我想我已经听全了，甚至听过了头。我不妨把话稍稍说在前头：此后整整四个月里——事实证明，那四个月便是我身为朗威尔顿教区牧师所余下的全部岁月——那一带的乡邻再没有向我提供过半点新的情况。我很快便想到：朗赫斯特那桩远在他乡、年深日久的谋杀，说不定反倒比这一桩新近犯下、为遮掩前者或许布置得更为周密的罪案，更容易寻出线索。我推想，彼得斯手里必已握有此案的证据；而在我翻检他那卷帙浩繁的文稿时，多半会浮出些什么，指点出那些证据的性质。于是，反正无论如何我本来也该这样做——我着手细读他的文稿。这件事占去了我闲暇中不小的份额，因为我发现，他那份预备立即付印的东西少得可怜，可为他计划中的那部心理学著作预备的材料，却比我依着他做事的做派所能料想的要完备得多、也珍贵得多。然而，对于眼下比我亡友的哲学更叫我关切的东西，我在这成堆的信札、笔记和日志里一无所获；所谓一无所获，是说没有一样能直接照亮这团疑云——因为说实在的，他那些心理学笔记，连同我与他的故友、牛津的一位哲学导师（我最终把笔记托付给了此人）的几番讨论，我想，倒确曾在此后我侦询工作的一个重要关节上影响我不浅。

要把查访推向更远的地方，就须有所审慎。稍有不慎，便可能让我正在做的事不必要地过早落到那个嫌疑人的眼里，而我归在他名下的那份大才干，说不定会替他筹划出某种有效的招数，来搅黄我的搜寻。不过，我自然及早给布莱恩斯顿先生去了信，问他可愿告诉我，他所暗指的"C."究竟是何人；朗赫斯特是否就是彼得斯疑为遇害的那个人；以及我推断"C."与此嫌疑有涉，是否推断得对。

===Original===
It was my duty to put all that I knew at the disposal of the police, and the opportunity for doing so soon presented itself in the visit, which, as I have said, was paid to me to enquire about Callaghan. My visitor was an important official, since dead, whom I need not more clearly indicate. He had been a military man, and he struck me as, in some ways, admirably qualified for his post. He was, I believe, excellent in the discipline he maintained among his subordinates and in all the dispositions he made for meeting the common public requirements. I am told also that he had wonderful familiarity with the ways of ordinary lawbreakers, but he did not appear to me to have much elasticity of mind. After answering fully his question about Callaghan, I thought it right to give my own impression of his innocence. My visitor answered me with a somewhat mysterious reference to those who really guided the conduct of the affair. He could not himself, he said, go behind their views. Then with an evident sympathy for my concern about Callaghan, he told me in confidence and still more mysteriously that the opinion of an eminent specialist had been taken.

I then ventured to press the question of Trethewy’s release, and learnt that it was being carefully considered, but he could not be set free immediately. Then I told my visitor of the statement of Vane-Cartwright when Callaghan first spoke of Trethewy, and how Trethewy’s confession proved this to have been a deliberate falsehood. I showed him and gave him copies of the letters of Bryanston to Peters and to me. I informed him, and at my request he noted, that Vane-Cartwright had opened the first letter. I stated what I had myself observed of Vane-Cartwright’s conduct, and indicated frankly the conclusion which I was disposed to draw. It did not seem to me that I produced any impression. My visitor listened, if I may say so, with the air of a man who completely takes in the fact and sees that it should be put in some pigeonhole, but is without either apprehension or wonder as to its real bearing. I gathered, on the whole, that the official mind was chiefly taken up with the theory that Callaghan was guilty; but that there was also thought to be an off-chance that something might yet turn up to repair the seemingly shattered case against Trethewy. I gathered too, and, I hope, gave due weight to the fact, that there was some likely way, of which I had before heard nothing, by which an unknown person might have entered and escaped from the house that night. One thing more I learnt; nothing suspicious had been discovered about Thalberg’s movements, but it appeared, and this seemed to be considered as in his favour, that he had a great deal to do with Vane-Cartwright.

===Chinese===
把我所知的一切交由警方斟酌处置，是我的本分；这样的机会很快便来了——正如我前面所说，有人来访，为卡拉汉的事向我查询。来客是位显要的官员，如今已经故去，我不必更明白地指明他是谁了。他行伍出身，给我的印象是，在某些方面堪胜其职。我相信，他在约束部下的纪律上、在应付公众日常需求的种种部署上，都出色得很。我还听说，他对寻常违法犯罪分子的门道熟稔之至；但在我看，他头脑的弹性却不甚了了。在就卡拉汉的问题作了详尽的回答之后，我觉得理应说出我自己认为他清白无辜的看法。来访者回答我时，隐约其辞地提了提真正主持此案办理的那些人。他说，他自己无法违拗他们的见解。接着，他显然出于对我那份为卡拉汉悬心的同情，又以机密而且更加玄乎的口吻告诉我：已经征询过一位杰出专家的意见。

随后我斗胆追问了释放特雷瑟维的问题，得知此事正在慎重考量之中，但他无法立刻获释。接着，我把卡拉汉初次提起特雷瑟维时韦恩-卡特赖特的那番陈述告诉了来访者，又说特雷瑟维的供词如何证明那是一句存心的谎言。我把布莱恩斯顿写给彼得斯和写给我的那两封信出示给他，并给了他抄件。我告知他——而且应我之请他记了下来——第一封信曾被韦恩-卡特赖特拆阅。我陈述了自己亲眼观察到的韦恩-卡特赖特的种种行迹，并坦率点明了我倾向于得出的结论。在我看来，我没有在他身上激起任何波澜。若容我打个比方，我的来访者听着，那神情就像一个人把事实囫囵收下，看出该把它归进某个格子里去，至于此事真正的分量何在，他却既无领会，也无惊异。总的听下来，官方的心思主要放在卡拉汉有罪的看法上；不过他们也觉得还有一线万一之机，说不定会再冒出什么，把那桩看似碎裂的对特雷瑟维的指控修补起来。我还听出——并且我希望，也给了应有的分量——这样一件事：兴许存在某条我从未听闻的路径，一个来历不明的人那晚可以由它潜入那座宅子再脱身而去。再有一件事我也得知了：关于塔尔贝格的行踪，没有查出任何可疑之处；但据说——而且这话似乎还是算作对他有利的一证——他与韦恩-卡特赖特过从甚密。

===Original===
After my visitor had taken courteous leave of me, it dawned upon me what was meant by his dark sayings about Callaghan. I had wondered how the opinions of an eminent specialist in police matters could be so cogent in a case about which he knew nothing at first hand. Suddenly it occurred to me that the eminent specialist really was a physician well versed in the symptoms of insanity. The police then were not being guided by those superficial and so to speak conventional notes of guilt, of which I had thought, to the exclusion to all those sides of character which I had noted. On the contrary they had a view of their own on which these two conflicting sets of phenomena might be reconciled, a view which explained why Callaghan was to me so inexplicable. The man was not sane.

I could not conceal from myself that there was at least something plausible in this view. There is a sort of marked eccentricity and, as it were, irresponsibility of conduct of which I had always thought as something not merely different from incipient madness but very far removed from it. Yet I had once before been terribly mistaken in thinking thus about a friend, and I might, I reflected, be mistaken now. The natural effect upon me was, or should have been, a keener sense of the unsubstantial nature of the story which I had built up about Vane-Cartwright. But I believed it still.

===Chinese===
来客对我彬彬有礼地告辞之后，我恍然醒悟他那些关于卡拉汉的玄言隐语是什么意思了。我原本纳闷：一位警事上的杰出专家，对一桩自己并无一手了解的案子，所下的断语何以能那样言之凿凿。猛然间我想明白了——这位杰出的专家，原是位深谙癫狂之症的医师。这么说，警方所倚为向导的，并不是我原先设想的那些浮面的、可说是照例行事的罪责标记——以致把我留意的那些性格侧面一概置之度外；恰恰相反，他们自有一套见解，足以把这两组互相冲突的现象调和起来；正是这套见解，解释了卡拉汉在我眼中为何如此不可索解。此人神智并不健全。

我无法向自己隐瞒：这种见解至少有几分道理。世上有一种惹眼的古怪、一种可称之为不负责任的行径，我一向以为，它与初萌的癫狂非但不同，而且相去霄壤。然而从前在一位朋友身上，我曾抱有这种想法而铸成大错；如今，我暗自思忖，说不定又错了。这番道理在我身上自然而然生出的效果——或者说本该生出的效果——是让我更痛切地觉察到，我围绕韦恩-卡特赖特构筑起来的那套说法，根基是何等虚浮。可我依然信它。

## XII

===Original===
In the course of the summer my wife and I paid our annual visit together to London, and I had a few days in Oxford before the end of the summer term.

I heard a good deal about Vane-Cartwright in London, for he had become a man of some mark in society, and moved in a little set, which was known among its members by a rather precious name, now forgotten though celebrated in the gossip of that time, and which included a statesman or two of either party and several men of eminence in letters, law or learning. By a strange coincidence of the sort which is always happening, I met at an evening party a friend who mentioned Longhurst, and I had just heard from him something of no moment about this man whose fate so deeply exercised me, when I saw Vane-Cartwright himself standing in another part of the throng. I took the opportunity of watching him, unobserved myself, as I supposed. I have hitherto forborne to describe his appearance, because such descriptions in books seldom convey a picture to me. But I must say that seen now in a room where there were several distinguished people, he made no less impression on me than before. He was, I should say, five foot eleven in height, thin and with a slight stoop, but with the wiry look which sometimes belongs to men who were unathletic and perhaps delicate when young, but whose physical strength has developed in after years. Hair which had turned rather grey, while the soft texture and uniformly dark hue of his skin still retained a certain beauty of youth, probably accounted for a good deal of his distinction of appearance, for he was not handsome, though his forehead, if narrow, was high, and his eyes which were small were striking—of a dark greenish-grey colour, I think. The expression of the mouth and of the clear-cut and firm-set jaw was a good deal hidden by a long though rather thin moustache, still black. I had time while he stood there to notice again one trick, which I already knew; he was, I supposed, bent upon being agreeable, so he was talking with animation, and when, in so talking, he smiled and showed his white teeth, his eyelashes almost completely veiled his eyes. To me, naturally, it gave him a hateful expression, yet I could see a certain fascination about it. Then he moved farther off—very quietly, but I could see as he made his way through the crowd that in reality every motion was extraordinarily quick.

===Chinese===
那年夏天，我和妻子照例结伴去伦敦小住；赶在夏季学期结束之前，我又到牛津盘桓了几日。

在伦敦，我听到不少关于韦恩-卡特赖特的议论，因为他已成了社交界小有名气的人物，出入于一个小圈子。这圈子在成员之间有个颇为故作风雅的名号，当年在街谈巷议中显赫一时，如今我已忘了；圈中有朝野两党的一两位政治家，还有几位文学、法律或学术界的显达。世上总在不断发生那种奇怪的巧事：在一次晚间聚会上，我遇到一位朋友，他提起了朗赫斯特——这个人的命运令我萦怀至深——我刚从他口中听完几件无关紧要的琐事，一抬眼，恰好看见韦恩-卡特赖特本人站在人群的另一头。我便趁此机会打量起他来，自以为不曾被他察觉。我至今一直按捺着不描摹他的相貌，因为书本里这类描摹很少能在我心头唤起一幅图像。但我必须说，此刻在坐了好几位名流的屋子里望见他，他给我的印象丝毫不减于从前。据我看，他身高五英尺十一英寸，清瘦，微微伛着背，却带着一股精瘦坚韧的劲头——这股劲头有时恰恰见于那样一种人：年轻时不好运动，兴许还身子单薄，体魄却在日后的岁月里才发育起来。头发已颇见花白，而皮肤的柔细质地与匀净的深色，犹自保留着几分青春的秀美，他仪表的出众大概多半由此而来；因为他并不英俊——尽管额头虽窄却高，眼睛虽小却惹眼，我想，是一种暗绿的灰色。嘴和那轮廓分明、咬合坚定的下颌所流露的神情，则大半被一部虽嫌稀疏、却蓄得长长的依然乌黑的髭须遮住了。他站在那里的当儿，我有工夫再次注意到他的一个小把戏，一个我早已知道的习惯：他想必正一心要讨人喜欢，所以谈得神采飞扬；而每当他这样谈着、微笑着露出白牙的时候，他的睫毛便几乎把双眼遮得严严实实。这在我眼里，自然给他添了一副可憎的神情，可我也看得出其中有种说不出的魔力。随后他往更远处去了——走得极轻；但看他穿行于人群之间，我明白，实际上他每一个动作都快得出奇。

===Original===
Some ten minutes after, I was about to go, when he suddenly came from behind and addressed me, asking me to choose a day for dinner or luncheon at his club. I declined, and freed myself as courteously and as quickly as I could, and thought, for the moment, that there had been nothing marked in the way in which, obeying irresistible impulse, I had shaken off the man whom I suspected on such slight grounds but so rootedly.

===Chinese===
约莫十分钟后，我正要离去，他忽然从背后过来同我搭话，请我择个日子，上他的俱乐部吃顿晚饭或午饭。我谢绝了，尽可能客气、也尽快地脱了身，一时间还以为，自己凭着一股按捺不住的冲动甩开此人的那副样子，并没有什么惹眼之处——我怀疑这个人，凭据实在微薄，怀疑却又根深蒂固。

===Original===
A few days afterwards a great robbery was attempted at Vane-Cartwright's house. The robbers were after a well-chosen and valuable collection of gold ornaments of early periods or from strange countries, which he had begun to make. It was reported in the papers that the theft had been with great presence of mind interrupted and prevented by the owner of the ill-guarded treasures. But the robbers themselves got away. The matter was much talked of, and conflicting tales were told about it, but it seems that Vane-Cartwright, hearing some unusual noise, had come downstairs and surprised the two men who had entered the house before they had succeeded in removing any of their spoil. As he came down he had rung up the police by the District Messenger Company's apparatus which was in the house. Coming quietly upon them, and standing in the dark while they were in full light, he had first ordered them to hold up their hands, and had then made each of them singly turn out his pockets and restore the smaller stolen articles which they had already secreted in them. He then, it was said, kept them standing there to await the police. But, by some ruse, they distracted his attention for a moment, and then, suddenly putting out their light, made a rush past him and escaped. Such at any rate appears to have been the information which he gave to the police who arrived soon after. The police actually arrested two men, already known to them as suspicious characters, who had been observed lurking near the house together and afterwards slinking away separately, and they were at first confident that they had secured the authors of the attempted robbery. But Vane-Cartwright not only could not identify the arrested men as the two housebreakers, whom he had of course seen well; he insisted firmly that they were not the men whom he had seen; nor were the right men ever caught. The matter caused some surprise, and the police were freely blamed for their bungling. I have my own reason for doubting whether they were justly blamed.

===Chinese===
几天之后，韦恩-卡特赖特家发生了一桩未遂的重大窃案。窃贼们图谋的，是一批拣选精当、价值不菲的金器收藏——上古时代或异邦国度的金饰——他搜罗这类东西才刚刚起步。报纸报道说，这批看守欠严的珍宝的主人临危不乱，及时打断并挫败了这桩盗窃。但窃贼们自己却脱身逃掉了。此事一时传为谈资，说法互相抵牾，不过经过似乎是：韦恩-卡特赖特听见某种异样的声响，下楼出其不意地撞见了那两个已经进宅的人，赶在他们弄走任何赃物之前。下楼途中，他已用宅中装设的区域信使公司（District Messenger Company）的装置唤来了警察。他悄没声地挨近他们，自己立在暗处，他们却在满屋灯光下一览无余；他先喝令两人举起手来，接着又命他们一个个单独翻出口袋，交还那些已经暗自揣进袋中的小件赃物。据说，随后他便押着两人站在那里等候警察。但两人用了个什么花招，一时引开了他的注意，随即猛地灭了灯，从他身旁夺路一冲，逃之夭夭。至少，他向随后赶到的警察所作的陈述似乎便是如此。警察果真逮捕了两名早已被他们认作嫌疑分子的家伙——有人看见这两人先是一同在宅子左近鬼鬼祟祟地转悠，随后又各自溜开——起初他们满心以为，那桩窃案的正主已经到手。可韦恩-卡特赖特不仅认不出被捕的两人就是那两个入室窃贼——那两人的模样他自然看得清清楚楚——更一口咬定他们绝非他见到的人；而真正的正主，也始终没有落网。此事引起了几分诧异，警察因办事笨拙而饱受指摘。这顿责骂是否骂得其所，我自有怀疑的理由。

===Original===
It is a mere fancy on my part that this incident and my meeting with Vane-Cartwright a few days before may have had a connection with each other and with certain subsequent events in this history. I fear that my experience in that year and the next has made me ready to see fanciful connections; and the reader, when he knows of those subsequent events, will see what I suspect took place upon the discovery of the theft, but will very likely think my suspicion extravagant. However that may be, Vane-Cartwright's plucky adventure and the celebrity which it helped to give to his artistic collections, caused me to hear all the more of him during my stay in London.

===Chinese===
这桩窃案与我几天前同韦恩-卡特赖特的相遇，说不定彼此关联，又与这段史事后来的某些变故相连——不过，这只不过是我个人的臆想罢了。我怕的是，那一年和次年的种种经历，已使我动辄看出些想入非非的关联；待读者得知后来那些变故，自会明白我疑心窃案被发觉的那一刻究竟出了什么事，只是多半会觉得我的疑心离奇过头。且不管究竟怎样，韦恩-卡特赖特这番胆气十足的历险，连同它帮着抬起来的他那些艺术收藏的名声，使我在伦敦逗留期间愈发络绎不绝地听到他的种种消息。

===Original===
Curiously, however, it was at Oxford, where he had not distinguished himself, that the fame of Vane-Cartwright was most dinned into my ears. The University is apt to be much interested in the comparatively few of her sons whose road to distinction is through commerce; and, moreover, he had lately given to the University Museum a valuable collection of East Indian weapons, fabrics, musical instruments and whatnot, which he had got together with much judgment. Thus it happened that I heard there one or two things about him which were of interest to me. A friend of mine, an old tutor, the Bursar of the college at which Vane-Cartwright had been, described him as he was in his undergraduate days. He had, in his opinion, been badly brought up, had never gone to school, but been trained at home by parents who were good people with peculiar views, highly scientific and possibly highly moral views. He had not fallen into either of the two common classes of undergraduates which my old friend understood and approved—the sportsmanlike and boyishly fashionable class, or the studious class who studied on the ordinary lines; still less into the smaller, but still not small, class which combines the merits of the two. He had attainments of his own, which the old tutor did not value sufficiently, for he was proficient in several modern languages and modern literatures; moreover, the necessary mathematics, Greek and Latin grammar, formal logic, etc., which he had to get up, gave him not the slightest trouble. Altogether he had plenty of cleverness of his own sort, but it was a sort which the Bursar thought unwholesome. He was quite well conducted, and ought to have been a gentleman, coming of the family of which he came, but somehow he was not quite a gentleman. Thus it was a great surprise to the possibly conventional instructor of his youth that he had done so well in the world.

===Chinese===
然而奇怪的是，偏偏是在牛津——他在那里并无所建树——韦恩-卡特赖特的名声灌进我耳朵里灌得最响。大学向来对那为数不多、经由实业成名的子弟格外留意；何况他新近还向大学博物馆捐赠了一批珍贵收藏——东印度的兵器、织物、乐器之类什物——都是他凭极高明的眼光搜罗起来的。于是，我在那里听到了一两件与他有关、令我觉得有意思的事。我的一位朋友——一位老导师，做过韦恩-卡特赖特就读那所学院的司库（Bursar）——向我描述了他本科时代的样子。在司库看来，他幼时的教养很糟：从未上过学，一直在家里由父母调教——父母是好人，却抱着些古怪的见解，极端科学的、或许还极端道德的见解。他不属于我那位老朋友所理解并嘉许的两类常见大学生——要么是爱好运动、孩子气地追赶时髦的一类，要么是循着常轨用功读书的一类；更不属于那人数较少、却也着实不少、兼采两类之长的第三类。他自有他自己的造诣，只是那位老导师未免看重不足：他精通好几门现代语言与现代文学；再说，那些必须突击应付的数学、希腊文与拉丁文文法、形式逻辑等等，也丝毫难不倒他。总而言之，他自有他那一套的聪明，而且绰绰有余；只是这一套，在司库看来是不健康的。他品行端正，又出身于那样的门第，本该是个绅士，可不知怎的，总差着一口气，算不得十足的绅士。于是，在这位或许流于守旧的少年时代的训导者眼里，他日后竟在世上混得如此得意，实在是一桩大大的奇事。

===Original===
Then I heard of him from another man, justly esteemed in financial circles, who was on a visit to his son at Oxford, and whom I met in a common-room after dinner. Somebody had hazarded the remark that Vane-Cartwright must have been either a very hard worker or a very lucky speculator. "No," said this gentleman, who was a colleague of his on the Board of one of the only two companies of which he was a director, "I should not say that a man like that worked hard as you would understand work at Oxford, or at least as a few of you would. His hard work was done when he was young. Most of his business is what one of his clerks could run, and probably does run, for many weeks together, on lines which he has planned very carefully and revises whenever occasion requires. Nor is he what most people would call a speculator. I fancy he very seldom takes any uncommon sort of risk, but he always does it at the right moment. He has succeeded because he is very quick in making his calculations and very bold in taking action on them. He does not seem to be constantly watching things, but when a special emergency or a special opportunity occurs he seems to grasp it instantly, and I believe he troubles himself very little, too little perhaps, about any affair of his when it is once well in train."

===Chinese===
随后，我又从另一个人那里听到他的事。此人在金融界素负清望，正到牛津探望儿子，我在晚饭后的公共休息室里遇见他。有人贸然放言，说韦恩-卡特赖特要么是个极其勤勉的苦干人，要么是个运气奇佳的投机客。「不对，」这位先生说——他是韦恩-卡特赖特的董事会同僚；韦恩-卡特赖特兼任董事的公司统共只有两家，此君便是其中一家的同僚——「依我看，那样一个人并不像你们在牛津所理解的那种用功，至少不像你们中间少数几位那样。他的苦功夫，年轻时就下完了。他的生意，大抵随便哪个办事员都能照着他精心筹划、遇有必要随时修订的章法，一连打理许多个星期——恐怕实际上也正是这样打理着的。他也不是大多数人所谓的投机客。依我看，他极少去冒什么不寻常的险，可一旦去冒，总挑在恰当的时刻。他之所以成功，是因为盘算得极快，而照着盘算动手时又极果敢。他似乎并不时时盯着行情，可一旦出现特别的急务或特别的机会，他仿佛转眼便能抓住；而且我相信，凡是他的一桩事业一旦上了正轨，他便极少再为它操心——也许操心得太少了。」

===Original===
Lastly, I heard a story, the narrator of which could give me few precise details, of the pains which Vane-Cartwright had taken to search out the few relations of an old partner of his in the East who had died before their affairs turned out so successfully, and of the generosity with which he had set up these people in life though they had very little claim on him. Here at least was something which took its place in the story which I was weaving; the rest of what I had heard was little to the purpose, though it served to give life and colour to my idea of the man's character.

Now, however, I was really to discover something definite. When we returned to our home at Long Wilton, only a little before we finally left it, I completed my examination of Peters' papers. His various diaries and notebooks, notes of travel and notes of study, jottings and completed passages for his psychological book, I found to be of fascinating interest, and I lingered over them long, but there was not a hint among them all of Longhurst, the *Eleanor* or any kindred topic. One of the journals, I noticed, had had some leaves cut out. The last place of my search was a small wooden trunk which I had brought home from his house (now sold). On the top of it lay a sheet of paper with, written in his mother's hand, "Some little things which I have put aside for Eustace. His wife or his children may care to see them hereafter." It may have been from a false sense of pathos, but my eyes filled with tears, and I was indisposed to rifle callously these relics so lovingly put aside with natural hopes which now could never be fulfilled. I was about to make a bonfire of the box and all its contents, reverently but with speed, when my wife arrested me in amazement at my folly. "Why," she said, "cannot you see? His letters to his mother will be in it." "His letters from the East," she added, as I still did not comprehend. And they were in it.

===Chinese===
最后，我还听到一个故事，讲故事的人说不出多少确切的细节：韦恩-卡特赖特早年在东方有位老合伙人，没等到两人的事业如此发达便去世了；他不辞辛劳寻访出那人寥寥几位亲眷，又慷慨解囊，替这些人安顿起生计——尽管他们实在没有什么名目受他这份恩惠。至少，这一件总算在我编织的故事里有了一席之地；其余听来的话大多文不对题，虽说也为我心目中此人的性格平添了几分生气与色彩。

然而直到这时，我才当真要发现一点确凿的东西了。我们回到朗威尔顿的家中——那时离我们最终离开那里已没有多少日子——我把对彼得斯文件遗物的检视做完了。他那些形形色色的日记和笔记本、旅行的札记与治学的笔记、为他那本心理学著作随手记下的片段和写成的段落，我读来只觉引人入胜，在其中流连许久；但这一切之中，关于朗赫斯特、*埃莉诺号*或任何相关的话题，没有一丝一毫的暗示。我还注意到，其中一本日记被人裁去了几页。我搜寻的最后一处，是一只小木箱，是我从他宅子里带回家来的（宅子现已售出）。箱面上放着一张纸，上面是他母亲的笔迹：「有些小物件，我替尤斯塔斯收着。日后他的妻子或儿女或许愿意看看。」也许是一种错发的感伤吧，我的双眼噙满了泪水，实在不忍冷漠地翻检这些遗物——它们被这样慈爱地收存在一处，寄托着如今再也无法实现的寻常心愿。我正打算把这只箱子和里面的全部东西付之一炬——恭恭敬敬，却手脚麻利——我的妻子却惊愕地拦住了我，怪我犯糊涂。「哎呀，」她说，「你怎么就想不到？他写给他母亲的信准在里头。」「他从东方写来的信，」见我仍旧不明白，她又补了一句。信果然就在里面。

## XIII

===Original===
I here set down in order of their date several extracts from Peters’ letters to his mother written from Saigon in the years 1878 to 1880.

First extract:

“I have a new acquaintance, one Willie Cartwright, a young fellow who was at Oxford just after me. I spend a good deal of time with him because of talking Oxford shop and because he is fond of books; at least he was brought up among them, and reads the books he thinks he ought to read. I have not got very much in common with him, for he is a narrow-shouldered, bilious-looking, unathletic fellow, with no instinct of sport in him; but he is a welcome addition to my circle, because he is refined—in a negative way at least—and most of my friends’ conversation here is—well, not refined, and it becomes a bore.”

Second extract:

“How curious that you should have known some of young Cartwright’s people, for it is W. V. Cartwright. I thought they must have lost their money since I heard of him at Oxford. Yes, I will try to ‘take care of him’ a little, as you say, but really, though he is quiet and not sociable among men, he is by no means a timid youth, and he has quite got the name of a shrewd businessman already.”

===Chinese===
我在此按日期先后，抄录彼得斯1878至1880年间自西贡（Saigon）写给他母亲的信中若干段摘录。

第一则摘录：

「我新近结识了一个人，名叫威利·卡特赖特（Willie Cartwright），是个刚在我之后上牛津的年轻人。我同他厮混了不少时光，一来为着说些牛津的行话，二来因为他喜爱书本；至少他是在书堆里长大的，读的书也都是他自认为该读的。我与他共同之处不多：他肩膀窄窄的，脸色青黄，一副不善运动的样子，身上没有半点运动的天性；不过让他进我的圈子倒也受人欢迎，因为他有教养——至少在消极意义上如此——而我此地大多数朋友的谈吐——嗯，谈不上有教养，听久了着实腻味。」

第二则摘录：

「真巧得很，你竟认识小卡特赖特家的一些亲眷，因为他正是W. V. 卡特赖特。我自从在牛津听说此人，便一直当他们家准是败了财。好吧，依你所言，我会尽力『照应照应』他；不过说真的，他虽沉静、不善与人结交，却绝不是一个胆怯的青年，而且早已得了个精明商人的名头。」

===Original===
Third extract:

“I am rather sorry about Willie Cartwright. He seems to have got into the hands of a fellow named Longhurst, who has lately turned up here, no one knows why. He, Longhurst, is a rough customer whom no one seems to know anything about, except that he has been in Australia. He has been a mining engineer, and seems to know also a lot about tropical forestry. He has wonderful yarns of the discoveries he has made in the Philippines, the Dutch Indies and all over the shop. I should not believe his yarns, but he seems to have made a little money somehow. Well, Cartwright now talks of becoming a partner with him in some wildcat venture, and I am afraid he will get let in. He says himself he thinks Longhurst will try to do him. He had much better stick to his humdrum business here, which will give him a living at any rate, and perhaps enable him to retire comfortably when he is, say, forty-five, young enough to enjoy life, though one does age soon in this climate.”

Fourth extract:

“Cartwright and Longhurst have actually gone off together. Parker, whom Cartwright was with, is very sick about it.  … By the way, I ought to confess I was quite wrong about Longhurst. I have seen a good deal of him since, and found him a very kind fellow, with an extraordinary simplicity about him in spite of all his varied experiences. I generally assume that when a man is spoken of as a rough diamond, the roughness is a too obvious fact, and the diamond a polite hypothesis, but I was wrong in Longhurst’s case. Also I think you may reassure C.’s aunt about the chances of his being swindled. In strict confidence I think the chances are the other way. MacAndrew, the lawyer here, told me a story he had no business to tell about the agreement between  …” (Part of letter lost.)

This was all. Peters before long was moved to Java; and the letters to his mother ceased soon after, for she died.

===Chinese===
第三则摘录：

「威利·卡特赖特的事，我有些替他担心。他似乎落进了一个名叫朗赫斯特的家伙手里；此人新近在此地露面，没人知道为着什么。这位朗赫斯特是个粗野的角色，除了去过澳大利亚，似乎谁也说不出他的底细。他当过采矿工程师，好像还懂得许多热带林业的学问。他有一肚子离奇的故事，讲他在菲律宾（Philippines）、荷属东印度（Dutch Indies）以及天南海北的地方作出的种种发现。换了我是不信那些故事的，可他不知怎的似乎真挣了一点钱。唉，卡特赖特如今竟说要同他合伙，搞一桩盲目的投机营生，我怕他要吃大亏。他自己也说，料想朗赫斯特会设法坑他。他远不如守着此地那份单调的营生：至少能给他一口饭吃，兴许还能让他到——比方说——四十五岁上舒舒服服地退下来，那时年纪还轻，尽够享受人生，虽说在这种气候里人老得快。」

第四则摘录：

「卡特赖特竟真的同朗赫斯特一道走了。原先与卡特赖特共事的帕克（Parker）为此懊恼得很。……对了，我该承认，我完全看错了朗赫斯特。此后我与他过从甚密，发觉他是个非常和善的人；尽管阅历五花八门，身上却有一种异乎寻常的单纯。我一向认定：一个人被说成浑金璞玉时，那『浑』是明摆着的事实，那『玉』不过是客气的假设；可朗赫斯特这一回，是我错了。另外，关于C.被人坑骗的可能，我想你可以让他的姑母宽心。说一句万不能外传的私房话：我看更可能被骗的是对方。此地的律师麦克安德鲁（MacAndrew）给我讲了一桩他本不该讲的事，说的是……之间所立的协议……」（信纸残缺。）

摘录到此为止。不久彼得斯便调往爪哇（Java）；写给他母亲的信也随即中断，因为她去世了。

===Original===
Not long afterwards I got Bryanston’s answer to my letter of enquiry to him. He told me little but things of which by this time I was sure. “C.” was Cartwright (William Vane-Cartwright, he called him), and was, he conjectured, the man whom Peters connected with Longhurst’s death. He would be glad to tell me at any time anything that he could, but he was off now for a sea voyage which the state of his health made necessary (a long absence immediately before accounted for some delay in his answering me), and at present he could think of nothing to tell me but what I should see in Peters’ letter to him, of which he was keeping the original and now enclosed a copy.

The important part of the letter enclosed was as follows: “I have a question to ask you which perhaps you will answer this time by return of post. Never mind my previous question about the old Assyrians. You will remember the time in 1882 when you were at Nagasaki, and you will remember Longhurst’s being there and his sailing. After his disappearance it got about naturally that he sailed in that unhappy ship the *William the Silent*, which went down in a cyclone.
